Pregnancy Cannabis Abuse Triples Postpartum Psychosis Risk

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

A massive population-level epidemiological study of more than 2 million California hospital deliveries reveals that women diagnosed with cannabis use disorder during pregnancy face a threefold higher risk of requiring emergency psychiatric care during their first year postpartum. Conducted by researchers at UC Merced, Michigan State University, and UCLA, and published in the Annals of Epidemiology, the investigation revealed that 10% of pregnant women with cannabis use disorder had an acute postpartum psychiatric emergency visit, compared to under 3% of unexposed mothers. Tracking deliveries from 2016 through 2021, coinciding with recreational cannabis legalization, the authors recorded a 40% surge in gestational cannabis use disorder diagnoses, highlighting an urgent public health need for routine clinical screening and evidence-based maternal mental health support.

Source: UC Merced / Annals of Epidemiology

As legal access to recreational and medicinal cannabis expands across North America, public perception of the drug has increasingly shifted toward regarding it as a benign, natural remedy. Driven in part by social media trends and commercial dispensaries marketing products for hyperemesis gravidarum (severe morning sickness), anxiety, and insomnia, an increasing number of expectant mothers perceive cannabis as a low-risk botanical alternative to conventional prescription psychotropics.

However, clinical reality often conflicts with popular perception. In a longitudinal epidemiological study analyzing state-mandated hospital discharge registries covering more than 2 million deliveries, researchers from the University of California, Merced, Michigan State University, and the University of California, Los Angeles, uncovered a strong association between problematic gestational cannabis consumption and severe psychiatric decompensation following childbirth.

Published in the Annals of Epidemiology, the research demonstrates that women diagnosed with cannabis use disorder (CUD) during pregnancy are more than three times as likely to require emergency department intervention for severe psychiatric disorders within the postpartum year compared to those without the disorder.

Led by Dr. Sidra Goldman-Mellor, associate professor of public health at UC Merced, and graduate researcher LaMecia De Alba, the team evaluated state hospital records from 2016 through 2021. The dataset stems from a National Institutes of Health (NIH) grant exploring the intersection of maternal substance use, violent victimization, and suicidal behavior across the perinatal spectrum.

The researchers analyzed three distinct maternal cohorts:

The findings demonstrated a stark clinical trajectory: 10% of women diagnosed with CUD during pregnancy required emergency department intervention for a psychiatric crisis during the 12 months following delivery.

By comparison, 15.6% of mothers diagnosed with alcohol use disorder experienced a postpartum psychiatric emergency, whereas fewer than 3% of the control population required emergency mental healthcare.

“One in 10 of the women who were diagnosed with cannabis use disorder during pregnancy went on to have a psychiatric emergency department visit in the postpartum year,” said Goldman-Mellor. “These aren’t just women saying, ‘I feel depressed.’ These are psychiatric symptoms serious enough that they had to go to the emergency room.”

The study’s chronological boundary, spanning 2016 to 2021, captured the critical public health shift that unfolded after California voters passed Proposition 64, which legalized recreational adult-use cannabis.

Over this six-year period, diagnoses of gestational cannabis use disorder rose by 40% across California delivery wards. In contrast, hospital diagnoses of alcohol use disorder among pregnant women remained flat across the same timeframe.

“It suggests there is a growing population of women who are using cannabis to a problematic extent when they’re pregnant, and that group is at higher risk of serious psychiatric problems,” Goldman-Mellor noted.

The authors caution that widespread commercial availability has outpaced public awareness regarding potential neurological and psychiatric vulnerabilities.

“Just because it’s legal doesn’t necessarily mean that it’s safe,” Goldman-Mellor explained. “It’s like alcohol: Many people use it safely, but there’s a subset who become addicted, and that has really negative repercussions for their lives. As cannabis becomes more socially accepted, we have to pair that with education about real risks and with systems that actually screen and support pregnant and postpartum women who need help.”

Expectant mothers navigating pregnancy-related mood disturbances or physical nausea often turn to cannabis believing that natural compounds carry zero pharmacology-associated side effects.

“Some individuals don’t want to take prescribed medications because they know the side effects. With cannabis, there’s not much stated about the actual side effects,” said co-author LaMecia De Alba, whose research investigates drug misuse patterns among structurally vulnerable groups. “Some people think that because it’s an herbal remedy, there are no larger side effects.”

The authors emphasize that because modern commercial strains, concentrated wax extracts, and vaporization cartridges contain tetrahydrocannabinol (THC) concentrations exceeding 70% to 90%, historical assumptions regarding cannabis potency are scientifically obsolete.

“There’s still a lot to be learned about the potency or the frequency of cannabis products that are being used within specific populations, especially among pregnant and postpartum women,” added De Alba.

The investigators explicitly note that the study design captures observational epidemiological correlations rather than direct biological causality. The dataset cannot definitively resolve whether heavy cannabis consumption directly disrupts postpartum neuroendocrine pathways, or whether cannabis misuse serves as a behavioral marker for underlying trauma, severe chronic stress, or unmanaged psychiatric comorbidities.

“This study is not designed to prove that cannabis use disorder causes psychiatric emergencies,” Goldman-Mellor emphasized. “We didn’t set out to prove causation. What we wanted to show is that pregnant women who are addicted to cannabis are not a neutral group — this is a high-risk group.”

Rather than instituting punitive legal sanctions that discourage pregnant women from seeking prenatal care, the researchers urge the healthcare system to implement universal, compassionate, and non-stigmatizing screening during routine obstetric visits.

Integrating early substance screening with tailored maternal mental health services could allow clinicians to identify high-risk individuals before discharge, preventing acute psychiatric emergencies during the vulnerable postpartum transition.

What is the connection between cannabis use disorder during pregnancy and postpartum mental health?

In a study of over 2 million California births, 10% of women diagnosed with cannabis use disorder during pregnancy suffered a psychiatric emergency requiring a hospital visit in their postpartum year. This represents more than triple the rate seen in women without the diagnosis (less than 3%).

Did cannabis use disorder become more common after recreational legalization?

Yes. Between 2016 and 2021, hospital diagnoses of cannabis use disorder among pregnant women in California increased by 40%, whereas rates of alcohol use disorder remained steady.

Does this study prove that cannabis causes postpartum psychiatric crises?

No. The study identified a strong epidemiological correlation, not biological causation. Women with cannabis use disorder represent a high-risk group that may also face higher rates of preexisting mental health conditions, social stress, or trauma, indicating a critical need for early medical screening and psychological support.

Prenatal cannabis use disorder and postpartum psychiatric emergency department visits: A retrospective cohort study of trends and associations in California, 2016–2021

Cannabis is the most-used substance during pregnancy and has known associations with psychiatric disorders (e.g., psychosis, depression). No prospective studies, however, have investigated associations between prenatal cannabis use disorder (CUD) and psychiatric outcomes in perinatal populations.

Annual cohorts of women with a live infant delivery in California from 2016 to 2021 were constructed using statewide linked hospital and emergency department (ED) discharge data. We examined temporal trends in prenatal CUD prevalence and compared risk of postpartum psychiatric ED visits between delivering women with prenatal CUD, prenatal alcohol use disorder (AUD; included as a benchmark exposure), and neither disorder.

The study sample included 2,036,016 delivering women. Between 2016 and 2021, prenatal CUD prevalence increased 1.0–1.4%. Postpartum psychiatric ED visits occurred in 10.4% of women diagnosed with CUD, 15.6% with AUD, and 2.7% with neither diagnosis. Relative to women with neither, those with CUD had ∼40% higher risk of any postpartum psychiatric ED visit (RRadj = 1.39, 95% CI: 1.32–1.47), while AUD was associated with 80% increased risk (RRadj = 1.80, 95% CI: 1.56–2.09).

Elevated risk of adverse psychiatric outcomes associated with CUD underscore the need for consistent screening, diagnosis, and treatment among perinatal women with CUD diagnoses.

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Spotify today expanded customization options for its AI DJ, allowing users to choose between different voices and personalities, as well as control how much the DJ talks between songs. Here are the details.

If you’re not familiar with Spotify’s AI DJ feature, the company describes it as “a personalized mix of music and commentary to help you discover new favorites and connect more deeply with the artists you love,” adding that it takes your listening history into account to “[bring] together your favorite tracks, songs you haven’t heard in years, and new releases to keep things feeling fresh.”

Spotify first introduced the interactive version of DJ in May 2025, and soon expanded its capabilities to Spanish-speaking users. Then, last May, Spotify expanded availability to more than 75 countries, alongside support for French, German, Italian, and Brazilian Portuguese.

Now, as spotted by TechCrunch, Spotify is introducing more customization options, including new voices and personalities, as well as the ability to reduce commentary between songs.

In a new post published on Spotify’s For the Record blog, the company announced today that “Spotify Premium listeners in markets where DJ is available can choose from an expanded selection of DJ voices in English and Spanish,” with seven new English options spanning British, American, Irish, Australian, and Indian accents, along with two new Spanish voices.

Additionally, Spotify now lets users choose whether they want commentary between sets of songs through a toggle that reduces the DJ’s interruptions to brief acknowledgments when starting a session or requesting different music.

The Role of the “Queen Mother” in Monarchies Around the World: Queen Sofía, Sonia of Norway, and Beatrix of the Netherlands

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Queen Sofía celebrated her 88th birthday on November 2 and remains a pillar of the Spanish royal family, with a demanding schedule that includes at least one official engagement per week. Queen Sofía has never set aside her institutional role, unlike some other European “queen mothers” (this official title is used only in the United Kingdom but is commonly used to refer to the mothers of kings), who, to a greater or lesser extent, continue to work for the monarchy in their respective countries.

This week, Queen Sofía will be in San Fernando, Cádiz, where she will serve as patroness of the ceremony to present the National Ensign to the Marine Corps, a special unit within the Navy and the world’s oldest marine corps, created in 1537 by Emperor Charles I. Far from retiring from public life, the Queen Emerita continues to play an important role within the Crown; she retains her office at Zarzuela and receives an official stipend.

She also remains closely involved in the day-to-day activities of the Queen Sofía Foundation and regularly visits the provincial offices of the Spanish Federation of Food Banks to learn about its charitable work, among other projects. Always discreet, Felipe VI’s mother has weathered the controversies surrounding Juan Carlos and maintained a professional image dedicated to serving the Spanish people—one that her family circumstances have not tarnished.

Queen Sonja, a great source of support today for her son, King Haakon.

Following the death of her husband, King Harald, in late August, and with the newly crowned Queen Mette-Marit on medical leave, Queen Sonja of Norway has become a pillar of support for her son, Haakon VIII. Sonia of Norway is 89 years old and in recent months has had minor health scares that led to her being hospitalized for a few days in the hospital. But despite everything, she continues her duties as a member of the Norwegian royal family. Just a few days ago, Queen Sonja traveled to Málaga to attend the opening of an exhibition on Picasso and Munch at the Picasso Museum in the Andalusian city, and was all smiles during a visit to a Norwegian school in Benalmádena.

On November 3, Queen Sonja accompanied her son Haakon to an official dinner to welcome the provincial governors at the Royal Palace—an important event at which the king will have his mother’s support in the absence of his wife and his daughter, Crown Princess Ingrid Alexandra, who is currently focused on her university studies in Oslo. Two days later, Queen Sonja will visit the Furniture Maker School in Askim, attend the opening of a furniture exhibition at the Center for Democracy at Fort Høytorp, and meet with members of the Indre Østfold Women’s Group and young people at the Re:læx youth center in Askim.

After more than three decades as queen, Sonia of Norway has stayed by her son’s side, at a time when he needs her support more than ever. Since her accession to the throne, Queen Sonia—whom Haakon stripped of the title “Her Majesty,” now simply Queen Sonia—has become a cornerstone of institutional and family support for the new monarch.

Beatrix of the Netherlands served as queen for 33 years.

At 88 years old, Beatrix of the Netherlands does not have a schedule as busy as those of Queen Sofía or Queen Sonja of Norway, but she has continued to attend institutional events and fulfill some official engagements since her abdication in 2013. King Willem-Alexander’s mother actively participates in award ceremonies linked to the Crown, such as the Appeltjes van Oranje awards presented by the Oranje Fonds foundation—awards whose bronze statuettes she designed herself and which she attends each year alongside King Willem-Alexander and Queen Máxima.

In addition, Princess Beatrix continues to make official trips to the Dutch Caribbean territories (such as her recent visits to Bonaire, St. Eustatius, and Saba) to preside over events dedicated to nature conservation, sustainable development, and education. She also continues to attend certain cultural and charitable events in her home country, such as the Dutch Ballet Gala hosted by the Dansersfonds Foundation, which she attended in mid-October.

Beatrix of the Netherlands, who now resides at Drakenstein Castle, was Queen of the Netherlands for 33 years, from 1980 to 2013. Following the coronation of her son Willem, she regained the title of princess, and today her official title is Her Royal Highness Princess Beatrix of the Netherlands, Princess of Orange-Nassau, and Princess of Lippe-Biesterfeld. Beatrix of the Netherlands remains deeply loved and respected by the people, and within the Orange-Nassau royal family, she continues to support and guide her son, King Willem-Alexander.

Princess Beatrix is a great role model for her granddaughter, Princess Amalia.

Queen Sofía, Queen Sonja of Norway, and Beatrix of the Netherlands all embody the key institutional values essential to preserving the monarchy in their respective countries. Loyal to the crown in one capacity or another and having dedicated their lives to public service for decades, all three are great examples of institutional loyalty for their granddaughters— Princess Leonor, Princess Ingrid Alexandra, and Princess Amalia—who will one day become queens.

Originally published in Vanity Fair Spain.

‘Landman’ Season 3 Is Filming at Taylor Sheridan’s 600-Acre Texas Ranch—Which He Previously Charged Paramount $50K a Week To Use

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Actor and director Taylor Sheridan has created an impressive slate of ranch-themed TV shows, but what is perhaps more remarkable is that some of them have been filmed on his very own properties.

The “Yellowstone” creator, 56, boasts an extraordinary property empire that’s estimated to be worth more than $600 million. And one of his most recognizable properties—the 600-acre Bosque Ranch near Fort Worth, TX—will reportedly be a filming location in Season 3 of the Sheridan-produced show “Landman.”

One of the series’ cast members, musician Clayton Q, confirmed the plans in a Nov. 6 Instagram post.

“Hey, y’all, Clayton Q here. I’m here at the world-famous Bosque Ranch right here in Weatherford, TX,” he said in the video. “Taylor Sheridan’s horse ranch. We got a lot of stuff going on. I’m on location today.”

Season 3 of the series began filming in October and is expected to premiere in mid-2027. It comes at a slight delay, as the season was green-lit in December 2025.

The Bosque Ranch Headquarters website confirms the legendary spot has been used to film a handful of projects, including “Yellowstone,” “Lawman: Bass Reeves,” and “Lioness.”

According to the Wall Street Journal, Sheridan had reportedly charged Paramount $50,000 a week to film at the ranch. His cattle was rented in herds, priced at $25 a cow.

Sheridan snapped up the property in 2013 for a whopping $264 million. At the time, the ranch served as a home base for his family, who had relocated to the Lone Star State from Los Angeles, where he previously worked as an actor on shows such as “Veronica Mars” and “Sons of Anarchy.”

Today, the home remains Sheridan’s primary residence, although he and his wife, actress-model Nicole Muirbrook, also spend time with their son at another property they own in Texas—the Four Sixes Ranch he purchased for $320 million in 2022.

“The main headquarters where we live is more of a horse ranch, because I’m pretty deep into the horse deal. And then the ranch out by Jacksboro is more where we run the cattle,” he explained in a 2020 interview.

Sheridan also has a $4.95 million ranch in Wyoming’s Star Valley, which he reportedly acquired in August 2024. The 179-acre homestead is known as Papa’s Creek Ranch and is located about 65 miles from Jackson Hole.

Despite his jaw-dropping portfolio, Sheridan hasn’t always been well-off financially. In an August 2026 interview with Town and Country magazine, he recalled a time when he was down on his luck.

“I was homeless twice,” he said. “I lived in my car. Sometimes I’d go to Ojai, just somewhere where I could safely sleep. I did that for six months.”

The multihyphenate noted that the struggle was technically his own choice.

“I could have decided at any point, ‘I’m going to get a full-time job and I’m going to give up this dream,’” he said of his acting career. “And I chose to sleep in my car over that.”

However, when he became more successful, after his stint on “Sons of Anarchy,” he developed a strategy to make sure he could solidly stand on his own two feet from there on out.

After turning to screenwriting, he sold his first screenplay for a drama called “Hell or High Water.” Afterward, he “bought a little house on an acre on a river in Wyoming.”

“We moved there, and I wrote a check for it,” he said. “Then, as I would start to have more success, I would buy a little bit more land beside it and get a couple horses. I’m still so averse to having to ever owe anybody anything.”

Sheridan explained that now that he’s built a career tied so deeply to ranching, he can avoid Los Angeles, a city he disliked living in.

Proximal Ventures raises debut fund to back startups bringing AI to drug development

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

While established venture firms continue to attract capital, first-time fund managers are facing one of the toughest fundraising environments in years. But that didn’t stop Sahir Raoof, a former SignalFire investor and executive at two healthcare startups, from raising $27.5 million for his first solo VC fund.

Raoof’s firm, Proximal Ventures, is betting that AI can transform the tools, infrastructure, and services that support drug development.

Pharmaceutical companies have historically outsourced various stages of drug development to specialized service providers, from synthesizing thousands of molecular compounds and figuring out how to manufacture drugs at scale to analyzing clinical trial data.

“For a long time, the assumption was that pharma is not a great buyer of software, but a really good buyer of services,” Raoof told TechCrunch.

Raoof believes AI could automate much of that work, creating an opportunity for startups to capture billions of dollars in pharmaceutical industry spending. By using AI, these companies could deliver the same services at a lower cost and in significantly less time, he said.

Some of the most valuable applications of AI so far have been in service-oriented industries like law and finance, Raoof said. He sees similar opportunities in pharmaceutical services.

Proximal plans to invest in pre-seed and seed-stage startups, writing checks from $500,000 to $1.5 million. The firm has already backed seven startups, including ChronicleBio, co-founded by former OpenAI executive Fidji Simo, which uses AI to analyze biological and medical data to better understand chronic diseases and identify potential treatments.

Before spending two years as a principal at SignalFire, Raoof led business development at clinical trial recruitment startup Iterative Health and previously oversaw partnerships at Flatiron Health, an oncology data company acquired by Roche for $1.9 billion in 2018.

“If you’re building a company that’s selling into the life sciences, one of the hardest things to do is starting that initial commercial flywheel,” Raoof said.

His sales chops, deep understanding of the healthcare ecosystem, and extensive industry relationships gave limited partners, the investors in VC funds, the confidence that he could spot strong startups and help them land customers. Proximal’s backers include VC firms SignalFire and Kleiner Perkins, Bertelsmann Healthcare Investments, and individual investors such as Dimension Capital co-founders Nan Li and Zavain Dar.

Amy Abernethy, co-founder of Highlander Health and former principal deputy commissioner of the FDA, is also an investor. She first met Raoof when they worked together at Flatiron Health, where she served as chief medical officer. She told TechCrunch that his ability to build relationships and spot industry bottlenecks made a lasting impression. Abernethy is convinced that his abilities, along with the understanding of what the healthcare market needs, will make him a successful investor.

It is these qualities that have helped Proximal invest in startups alongside some of the better-known, much larger funds, she added.

Nvidia Erases 2026 Gains as Republicans Threaten Jensen Huang With Subpoena

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Nvidia Erases 2026 Gains as Republicans Threaten Jensen Huang With Subpoena

Nvidia shares plunged to $166.95 Tuesday afternoon, leaving the AI chipmaker down 18.42% for November and wiping out all the gains it had accumulated in 2026.

The selloff accelerated as congressional Republicans threatened to subpoena CEO Jensen Huang over his reported relationship with a suspected Chinese intelligence operative.

At that price, Nvidia was roughly 10.5% below its $186.50 closing price at the end of 2025 and 31.4% below its recent $243.37 peak. November is shaping up as its worst month since September 2022, when shares lost roughly 19.6%; the company suffered an even deeper 32% crash in April of that year.

Tuesday's drop exceeded 6% in afternoon trading, after a 4.3% fall on Monday. The two-day decline was on track to erase more than $470 billion in market value. Even after the significant drop, some analysts would still view the stock as extremely overbought in the long term, with prices much higher than what would be considered safe based on technical indicators.

Just for context, even an additional 20% crash (-43% from October) would be considered healthy in an isolated technical analysis, leaving the price still above the average price of the last 50 months. Prices tend to either bounce or trade near that line. When prices go too far away from the average, that usually means there is an unusual appetite for the asset, typically driven more by FOMO than fundamentals.

House Foreign Affairs Committee Chairman Brian Mast told ABC News on Tuesday that he was prepared to subpoena Huang unless the CEO responded by Wednesday, November 11, to a request for a closed-door briefing. No subpoena had been issued at the time of the report.

Mast and two other Republican committee chairs asked Huang to appear on November 18 and explain his interactions with Alice Huang, a former executive at Singapore-based chip buyer Megaspeed. The lawmakers also want details about Nvidia's safeguards against foreign espionage, according to a letter reviewed by Reuters.

U.S. authorities have reportedly investigated whether Alice Huang, who has no family relationship to the Nvidia CEO, passed information to Beijing. The allegations have not established that Jensen Huang knowingly disclosed confidential information or violated export rules.

On Sunday, Nvidia's board announced an independent investigation into whether its chief executive was targeted by a foreign intelligence operation. The board said it had no reason to believe that national security or company secrets were compromised, but would examine the available information.

Earlier today, the Financial Times reported Nvidia has hired former U.S. prosecutor Joon H Kim to conduct the internal investigation.

The company is also facing legal pressure. Shareholders filed a proposed class-action lawsuit Monday alleging that Nvidia and Huang failed to disclose risks associated with the reported relationship.

The scandal has landed during a broader reassessment of the AI boom. Investors have questioned how long massive spending on data centers can continue, and Nvidia's competitors are offering customers more alternatives to its expensive chips.

Google introduced new AI chip configurations Monday. The Information reported that OpenAI was considering buying Google's tensor processing units, or TPUs, which are processors built specifically to run AI workloads. Neither company has confirmed a purchase but yesterday Google announced a new line of TPUs available for select customers, directly competing against Nvidia’s chips.

Nvidia is scheduled to report quarterly earnings on November 17, one day before the congressional briefing requested by House Republicans. Mast's deadline for Huang to respond comes first, on November 11.

Tesla Cybertruck had its best month in over a year. It’s still going away

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

New EV prices edged up in October, but buyers still paid less than they did a year ago. The average transaction price for a new EV in the US was $56,220, up 1% from September and down 2.7% from October 2025, according to Kelley Blue Book, a Cox Automotive brand.

Across all new vehicles, the average hit a record $51,109, up 1.3% from September and 2.1% year over year, even as prices rose more slowly than they typically do in October.

EV incentives barely budged from September’s revised 11.2% of the average transaction price to 11.3% in October. That’s up from 10.3% a year earlier and well above October’s industrywide average of 6.8%.

Tesla’s average selling price fell from September to $54,194, but it was still up 1.2% year over year. The Cybertruck helped push that average higher. Cox Automotive estimates Tesla sold 1,914 Cybertrucks in October, its best month in more than a year, at an average price of $92,380.

VSCO Updates Its Policy to Explicitly State It Won’t Train AI on Your Photos

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

VSCO Updates Its Policy to Explicitly State It Won’t Train AI on Your Photos

This, of course, set off some alarm bells and the company quickly said it wouldn’t be training AI models on users’ content. For some, this might not have been enough, however, and VSCO seems to have taken note of that.

“You deserve a straight answer about what happens to the work you put on VSCO,” Eric Wittman, CEO of VSCO, writes in a letter sent to VSCO users today. “Sharing your work shouldn’t mean wondering whether it will be used to train a system designed to generate content (photos or video) without you.”

As a result, the company has today updated its Creator Content Standards to more explicitly state this stance.

“We don’t use your content to train generative AI models. Not your public work. Not your private work. Not because you have a paid membership. The same protection applies whether you pay us or not,” Wittman continues.

“We’re updating our Creator Content Standards to make that explicit.”

The updated Content Creator Standards page now says the following:

If you are a paid member with an active VSCO membership or a non-paying Creator on VSCO, your Public or Private Creator Content will not be utilized to develop, train, or improve AI or machine learning models for Generative AI Use.

“For the past 15 years we have championed photographers. That means building a business around helping you make your work and earn from it. It doesn’t mean treating the photographs you entrust to us as raw material for models that generate images, video or other media. It’s a good moment to also be precise about what we mean by generative AI because AI isn’t one thing, and calling out the distinction matters,” he writes.

“We use machine learning technology and non-generative AI across the VSCO platform with the goal of helping photographers, whether that’s to find work, search for people to connect with, edit images and get more out of our products. We have heard from the photography community this is where AI is useful. That’s very different from using your photographs to train models that generate new content. Our updated standards make that boundary explicit.”

VSCO says that it isn’t just holding itself to these standards, but anyone the company works with, too.

“We’re holding our providers to that distinction, too. Outside AI providers who have been powering VSCO features have already agreed not to use content submitted through our services to train generative AI models. Language in our previous Creator Content Standards was too broad to make these distinctions and intentions clear. That left room for confusion about where we stood. You shouldn’t have to decipher a policy to find out what is happening with your work on a platform you use,” Wittman says.

“Your work isn’t just a file on our platform. It represents the time you spent learning, the places you went to, the people who trusted you and the choices only you could make. That perspective and what you choose to do with it is yours.”

VSCO’s updated Creator Content Standards can be read on the company’s website.

What Gut Health Actually Means, In A World Where The Algorithm Is Constantly Shilling Supplements

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Here’s what experts actually look for, recommend, and see as red flags.

Nervous system regulation, sleepmaxxing, high-functioning burnout — there is always some hot new health topic du jour. While there’s usually some kernel of true health information buried in the hype, it’s hard to parse what really matters from all the social media “gurus” shilling supplements at the link in bio. This is especially true of “gut health,” a topic that feels like it has dominated wellness spaces for the past few years but also never really comes up in your primary care doctor’s office. So, what does gut health actually mean, particularly for women? What do we need to know, and what is social media fluff?

We’ve heard the term; what does it actually mean? In general, gut health refers to the state of your gastrointestinal tract and how it’s working for you, according to the American Medical Association (AMA). Regular bowel movements, no persistent pain or bloating — these are signs things are going well. Medically speaking, good gut health doesn’t mean you’ve somehow optimized your microbiome (more on that in a minute); it means your digestive tract is functioning as it should, you’re absorbing the nutrients you need, and you’re comfortable throughout the process.

“I think that it looks different for everybody, but when I have a healthy gut, I feel better overall in my day-to-day life. It’s no bloating on a consistent basis. It’s having regular bowel movements. I’m not racing to the bathroom with loose stools or, on the other side of it, I’m not going days constipated. I don’t feel that brain fog. I have energy, and I’m sleeping well,” says Chloe Melas, NBC News correspondent and author of The Girl’s Guide to Guts, who lives with ulcerative colitis, a type of inflammatory bowel disease (IBD).

Yes, it is a real thing and the subject of reams of medical research.

Inside your intestines live trillions of microorganisms that have a surprising impact on our overall health. According to The Cleveland Clinic, they help break down our food, support our immune systems, and may even play a role in mood and behavioral health. There’s no one perfect gut microbiome either, according to the AMA. Each person’s is unique and depends on their age, diet, medications, genetics, where they live, and more.

It’s normal to have an off tummy day every now and then, sure, but from her own experience and speaking with experts while writing her book, Melas knows there are certain red flags women should watch for when it comes to their gut health.

“If you’re having blood in your stool on a consistent basis, and that more than a handful of times, you need to go see a doctor immediately. That can look like going to see your primary care. Personally, in my experiences in writing this book and interviewing so many different experts, and just what I've gone through in my own life having IBD, I’d say go straight to a gastroenterologist,” Melas says.

Vomiting, unexplained weight loss, chronic stomach pain, and difficulty swallowing are also red flags, per the AMA. Inconsistent bowel movements, whether that means racing to the bathroom multiple times a day or being chronically constipated, are another reason to schedule an appointment with your doctor, she adds. Cramping, painful bloating, or looking pregnant after eating a meal — these things are not normal, says Melas.

Ignoring early signs of GI issues can keep you from feeling your best, and in some cases, missing a diagnosis you need. Women are twice as likely as men to have IBS, according to the American College of Gastroenterology.

“For so long, so many of us have just sort of been like, ‘This is just the way life is.’ I think that as women, we just don’t prioritize our health like we should, especially when it comes to our gut health. We’re really good about getting our mammograms and talking about our breast health. We are now finally talking about perimenopause and those symptoms. But 45 million Americans suffer from IBS. Nearly 4 million Americans suffer from inflammatory bowel disease, which raises your chances of having colon cancer. And these are things that require a doctor to help you diagnose.”

Probiotic sodas, gummies, powders... what do experts say will actually help improve your GI health and comfort? What will actually make your microbiome happy, and what’s not worth the money? Health experts agree that a diet rich in fiber is the best way to support your gut’s health.

“I had the privilege of interviewing Dr. William Lee, and he wrote a famous book called Eat to Beat Disease. He said that you have 39 trillion different bacteria that live inside your microbiome and that you need this gut diversity to have overall good health, and that it really just comes down to having a diverse plate every day when you’re sitting down for your meals,” Melas says.

It’s generally recommended for adults to consume 25 to 30 grams of fiber per day, which Melas admits can be hard sometimes with diet alone. That may mean a fiber supplement is worth the investment for you, or it may mean snacking on more fruits and veggies throughout the day where you can.

“We all get so overwhelmed; we’re constantly being targeted by drink this, eat that, take this supplement, take these vitamins. But Dr. Lee says that really what you need is in the front of the grocery store. It's the fruits, it's the vegetables, and that it's the things that are easiest to reach first,” Melas adds.

Ultimately, Melas says she wants more women to prioritize their health, and not to be embarrassed or afraid to talk to their providers about GI health specifically.

“If you are not having regular bowel movements, and by that I mean a big, beautiful, healthy poop every single day, then something is off with your gut health, full stop, and you need to get it figured out,” she says. “If you are bloated all the time, if you feel like every time you eat that cheese or a certain meal that you all of a sudden look like you’re three months pregnant, you’re not imagining it and you’re not crazy. We’ve just been told for so long to normalize it, and it's embarrassing, and it's not sexy, and who wants to sit around with your girlfriends at night and talk about the consistency of your stools? But we need to be talking about it because, honestly, it could save lives.”

X-energy gets green light to build its first small modular nuclear reactors

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Nuclear startup X-energy announced Monday that it had been given the go-ahead from federal officials to build its first four commercial reactors.

The Nuclear Regulatory Commission (NRC) approved the construction permit application for four of X-energy’s Xe-100 reactors at a Dow chemical plant in Texas. Each reactor will be capable of generating 80 megawatts of electricity and 200 megawatts of heat. X-energy says the approval is the first for an advanced nuclear reactor at an industrial site in the United States.

The U.S. nuclear industry has pinned its hopes on small modular reactors — or SMRs — like those being developed by X-energy. By shrinking the reactor, proponents say it will speed construction and reduce costs, though those advantages have yet to be proven. No SMR is currently in operation in the U.S., and X-energy says Dow’s reactors won’t be operational until the early 2030s.

X-energy claims that its reactor design, which flows helium gas over uranium-containing fuel pellets, is safer than many existing reactors, but the Union of Concerned Scientists questioned the claim and the speed with which X-energy received the permit.

“The NRC only accomplished this feat by pushing consideration of critical safety questions to a later date—issues the NRC should’ve fully resolved before green lighting construction,” Edwin Lyman, director of nuclear power safety for the organization, said in a statement.

The nuclear industry has benefited from strong interest from hyperscalers and data center developers, who are desperate for any and all sources of electricity. Amazon’s Climate Pledge Fund led X-energy’s $500 million Series C-1, and the big tech company has also committed to buying 5 gigawatts of capacity from the nuclear startup.

X-energy rode that wave of excitement earlier this year when its April initial public offering raised $1 billion. But since then, investors’ optimism has tempered.

The hesitancy isn’t surprising: Nuclear power plants are expensive to build and can take years to complete. Eventually, X-energy hopes to rein in both time and cost, but its first reactors are likely to be pricey.

The new permit approval gave X-energy a small jolt yesterday, though it was only enough to recover last week’s losses. The company’s shares are trading at less than half their initial offering price.

Watch BYD’s robotic charging arm plug itself in and charge EVs in 5 minutes [Video]

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

BYD’s Flash Charging tech can charge an EV in as little as five minutes. Now, BYD is taking things a step further with integrated solar power and a robotic arm that plugs itself in. See how it all works in action.

After unveiling the Yangwang Altior in the UAE on Monday, its new ultra-luxury flagship electric sedan, BYD showcased its latest breakthrough technology.

The Chinese automaker launched a new integrated charging system in the Abu Dhabi desert that brings BYD’s “Three Green Dreams” to life.

In 2006, BYD president and CEO Wang Chuanfu revealed his vision of “Three Green Dreams,” including harnessing solar power to generate renewable energy, storing that energy in batteries, and replacing gas-powered vehicles with electric ones that run on clean energy.

The new charging system is the “world’s first,” according to BYD, to integrate solar power generation, energy storage, Flash Charging, and robotic charging together in one solution.

BYD’s Flash Charging tech enables a vehicle’s battery to charge from 10% to 70% in five minutes, essentially the same time it takes to fill up at a gas station. For a full recharge, or to go from 10% to 97%, it takes just nine minutes.

The new robotic charging arm automatically locates the vehicle’s charging port, opens its cover, plugs in, and then removes it once the session is over.

By combining robotic charging with its Automated Valet Parking, BYD said the new tech goes beyond automated driving and parking to the charging process itself.

Once the vehicle is parked, the robotic charging arm automatically takes over and connects it to Flash Charging without the driver even touching the cable.

A post shared by Michael Martinez (@mmichael_martinez)

Michaël Martinez, head of marketing at BYD France, posted a video (shown above) showcasing the new charging tech in action.

The integrated solution is built around BYD’s Solar-Storage-Charging (SSC) system, fusing solar energy generation, battery storage, and its 5-minute Flash Charging tech. Together, the setup captures energy from the sun, stores it, and distributes it as needed.

If they could tie this into a smart summon type of capability, where the vehicle communicates with the charger and moves the vehicle into the proper location for charging and then moves to a parking spot once charging is complete. Rental car fleets would benefit and so would those in shopping centers. There are so many possibilities and this looks far more mature that the old snake charging Tesla demoed.

BYD has over 16 years of experience in the solar industry with a portfolio that includes solar cells, modules, and systems.

Its solar division, BYD Solar, does business in over 100 countries and regions. By using solar power, BYD said, the SCC system can operate independently off-the-grid.

The new technology is part of BYD’s wider Flash Charging rollout. By the end of 2027, the company plans to deploy 6,000 Flash Charging stations globally.

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Photo Credit: Group of Italian tourists in Shibuya, Japan.  Wikimedia Commons / Syced

Research shows the U.S. losing ground to Japan as costs and politics turn travelers away. Brand USA is betting the lost demand is deferred, not gone, but its budget to win it back is shrinking.

The U.S.’ place as international travelers’ top destination is slipping as more key markets look to Japan and other destinations, according to new research from Brand USA shared at its board meeting Tuesday.

While the U.S. once held a comfortable lead over competing destinations, that gap has narrowed as Japan gains ground and interest in the U.S. wanes. In January of last year, the U.S. led Japan by more than eight percentage points as the top destination. In August and October 2026, Japan finished ahead of the U.S.

New Brand USA research presented at its board meeting Tuesday shows the U.S. losing its long-held lead as the destination international travelers are most likely to visit. U.S. consideration fell from 30% in 2019 to 24% year to date in 2026, while Japan climbed to 23% and finished ahead of the U.S. in both August and October 2026. Researchers found a way to grow full size broccoli under solar panels while making electricity at the same time

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Under magenta solar panels passing a fraction of the sunlight, broccoli reached full head weight 25 days late while the panels made power.

Placing solar panels over a garden blocks the sun. That normally harms plant growth since most crops require plenty of light. However, that has proven not to be the case under all circumstances.

A new study shows that growing broccoli under special pink solar panels actually helps the plants use sunlight more efficiently.

While it took the broccoli an extra 25 days to mature, they still grew to full size because the semi-transparent panels were designed to let through the best red and blue light for growing.

This setup could allow farmers to harvest both crops and renewable energy from the exact same field.

Silvia Ma Lu of Mälardalen University in Västerås, Sweden, led the work. Her co-authors came from the same University and from two Italian institutes, the Institute for Sustainable Plant Protection in Florence and Università Cattolica in Piacenza.

“The basic concept is quite straightforward,” Ma Lu said. “The solar panels use part of the incoming sunlight to generate renewable electricity while allowing part of the light to pass through to the crops growing underneath.”

A leaf absorbs blue and red light and reflects more of the green, which is why plants look green. A magenta filter does the opposite, passing red and blue and blocking green.

So a magenta solar panel lets through the colors that a crop, such as broccoli, uses and absorbs the rest to make power.

Each panel is a sheet of glass with thin strips of a light-absorbing material, cadmium telluride, spaced across it. A magenta plastic layer inside the glass tints the light that passes between the strips.

Growing crops under panels is called agrivoltaics, and most projects use dark solar panels that cast heavy shade.

Ma Lu’s team built two canopies, each about 13 by 16 feet (4 by 5 meters) and 5 feet (1.5 meters) tall at the low edge.

One used panels the maker rates as 50 percent transparent, the other 70 percent. Under them, broccoli received about 13 and 19 percent, respectively, of the growing light that reached a third, open plot.

Seedlings went into all three plots at Kärrbo Prästgård, a farm in central Sweden, on July 2, 2024. There were thus 44 plants per plot, under white insect netting.

The open-field broccoli was ready to cut after 79 days. At that point the plants growing under the panels were shorter, with smaller heads and thinner stems, so the team let them keep growing.

By day 104, the heads under both canopies weighed about the same as the open-field heads had at day 79. Whole plants in the open still weighed more.

The shaded plants had grown 25 to 41 percent more leaves than their neighbors in the sun. No plant in any plot developed a hollow stem, a defect that the team checked for.

A later harvest could also land in weeks when fresh broccoli sells for more, the authors noted.

Full sun at midday delivered about 925 units of growing light to the open plot, counted as photons per square meter per second.

The canopies passed 143 and 207 of those units, yet the shaded broccoli produced nearly as much plant matter. Each unit of light yielded up to 4.5 times more dry weight than it did in the open.

Single leaves showed the same pattern under a lamp. Leaves from under the panels photosynthesized faster at every light level than leaves from the open plot.

On sunny days the open-field plants were likely getting more light than they could use, Ma Lu’s team wrote. The shaded plants had adjusted to capture more of the little light they did get.

Afternoons under the panels were about 1.8°F (1.0°C) warmer than in the open plot, and nights about 2°F (1.1°C) cooler. The air was also about 6 percent more humid, so the shaded plants lost less water.

Rain was plentiful from September on and, with drip irrigation before that, soil moisture was not what limited either plot.

At the first sampling, iron in the shaded heads was 37 to 41 percent lower than in open-field heads, and manganese was 38 to 42 percent lower. Zinc was down 18 to 20 percent, copper 12 percent, and dissolved sugars by a third to a half.

Calcium, magnesium, and potassium rose instead, 25 to 54 percent higher under the panels in September. By the second harvest those three had fallen back to match the open-field heads, while the four trace metals stayed low.

“One of the most interesting findings was how similarly the broccoli performed under the two solar panel systems despite their different transparency levels,” Ma Lu said.

“However, these findings are specific to our experimental conditions and should be validated across additional growing seasons and system configurations.”

The denser canopy generated an estimated 447.6 kilowatt hours over the season from panels rated at 1.1 kilowatts, converting 7.2 percent of the sunlight hitting them to electricity. Standard silicon panels convert 20 percent or more.

On paper, the team also scaled the numbers up. By that estimate, 2.5 acres (1 hectare) under these panels would produce as much food and power as 2.9 acres (1.18 hectares) split between a field and a solar farm.

“Configurations similar to our prototype may currently be suitable for smaller-scale applications, such as community gardens, or for integration into greenhouse roofs rather than immediate deployment over large agricultural areas,” Ma Lu said.

Each treatment had one plot, six sampled plants, and a single season in central Sweden behind it. Plants at the canopy edges got extra sun and may have shaded or sheltered the ones measured in the middle.

In the 25 days after the open-field harvest, those plants could have grown side shoots for a second cut that the study did not weigh.

The study also can’t separate the magenta color from plain shade or from the softer, scattered light under glass.

Ma Lu’s team has begun testing the magenta panels, along with red and blue ones, under lab lighting where no outside light leaks in.

The full study was published in the journal Cell Reports Physical Science.

Virgil van Dijk issues transfer latest as Liverpool contract expiry looms

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Virgil van Dijk maintains that he is relaxed about his Liverpool contract situation.

The Dutchman’s deal at Anfield expires at the end of the current campaign, and there have been no indications so far that he will sign an extension.

The central defender has been one of the Reds’ first-choice players ever since he arrived at Anfield back in 2018, joining from Southampton for £75million.

The No4 has taken on the captain’s armband on Merseyside following Jordan Henderson’s departure, with the 35-year-old racking up 282 appearances for the club since his move.

Now, he has issued an update on his contract status, insisting that he is not panicking over his future.

"I feel good, but it’s not about me at the moment," the centre-back said. "Let’s see. I’m not in any panic whatsoever. I want to keep playing and continue what I’m doing.

"Let’s see what the season brings. I’ve had an amazing time already so far. If that will be extended, that’s the case.

"If not, then I will go somewhere else. There’s nothing to report at the moment, but that doesn’t mean that there’s any stress or anything."

Van Dijk withdrew from the Netherlands’ Nations League camp with an injury - Liverpool confirmed on Monday - and manager Andoni Iraola will be hoping that the issue is minor, meaning that his skipper is able to face Manchester United on November 22.

"Physically I’ve been struggling with a little niggle the last couple of weeks but I do everything in my power to be out there," Van Dijk added.

‘I Signed Up for a Marathon at the Worst Possible Time—And It Was the Best Decision I Made’

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

“If you wait until you feel ready, you’ll be waiting forever.”

I wasn’t planning on running a marathon this year, let alone the New York City Marathon. Between its notoriously hilly course, relentless bridges, and the logistics of traveling across the country from my home in Colorado, NYC wasn’t exactly the easiest to take on. I’m also a busy mama to a 3½-year-old and 2-year-old, I work full time (I am the founding coach of the Ladder app and coach Team Body and Bell), and I had sinus surgery about six months ago. A marathon definitely wasn’t on my bingo card among the trenches of motherhood—but it happened.

After a really healthy and injury-free training block, I made it to the starting line. It was time for me to have my redemptive victory, but instead, I rolled up with the flu. At the time, I had no idea, and I chalked up how lousy I was feeling to the exhaustion of traveling with two little kids, lack of sleep, and all the chaos that comes with it. It wasn’t until I got home and was diagnosed that I realized what I’d actually been dealing with on race day. From the starting line, I found myself self-loathing and robbed of the marathon experience I’d worked so hard for.

But little did I know, the setbacks and imperfections of race day and training were actually going to allow me to experience the marathon even more than if I had been chasing a pace and finish time.

Between two pregnancies and postpartum periods, a C-section, and other unplanned health setbacks, it had been a difficult few years. I felt constantly defeated. Even as someone who works in fitness, I felt like I hadn’t done much to take care of myself in quite some time.

When the marathon opportunity presented itself, there was something almost rebellious about being able to choose my own hard in spite of the daily challenges that were out of my control as a mom.

As I was flooded with mom guilt, I kept asking myself: When will it be a good time to do this? When is it a good time to do anything for yourself as a mom? The answer was a resounding never. So I decided to rip off the bandaid, find a bib (thank you, Gatorade!), and fully commit to a three-month training block with a coach. (I hired a Body and Bell member who had actually trained with me since the start of the app. She is a seven-time NYC Marathoner and the most passionate running coach I know. Check her out on Instagram: @lindaleighlore.)

My life did not stop during this time. Work was more demanding than ever. We went on two family vacations. The kids got me sick multiple times. The training conditions were never perfect—but I did manage to get the mileage in. I reminded myself of something I say as a coach all the time: “It doesn’t need to be perfect, it just needs to be done.”

Let me preface something important as someone who works in fitness: endurance training was very new to me. I am a speed, power, and strength girlie at heart. The three-hour-plus long runs, pounding the pavement and moving slower, was very mentally unstimulating and out of my comfort zone.

But this new sport allowed me to enter a headspace I had never experienced before. There was something so recharging about being able to leave the house, put my phone in my pocket, and be in my own thoughts that healed me in ways I didn’t know I needed to be healed. Training did not only provide a break from the kids, but it also gave me a space to allow me to become more available for them.

In the fitness world, running and strength training are often viewed as two completely different styles of training. We have strength versus cardio zealots. But what more people need to understand is how much they thrive together.

The aerobic engine I was building with running gave me more cardio capacity for my lifts (lifting heavier felt smoother than it ever had) and all the muscle I had gained over the years made me an extremely strong and resilient runner (I recovered so well in between runs).

I felt structurally and aerobically unstoppable.

When I got to the start line, I knew something was off. I grew up as an athlete and knew what to expect with the competition nerves—but this was different. I was coughing, had a sore throat, and a raging headache. I kept telling myself it would all go away once I started moving. And for a while, it did. But around mile six, somewhere deep in Brooklyn, the symptoms came back with a vengeance, along with full-body chills.

It was way too early to be “bonking” in a marathon, and I was confused and simply annoyed. I tried to stay toward the middle of the street, away from the crowds. I purposely didn’t wear headphones to take in the crowd’s energy, yet there I was, trying to hide from it.

I crossed the Queensboro Bridge into Manhattan, where I knew my family was waiting for me at mile 17. I was so excited to see them, but I was also dragging so badly by this point, and I needed to take a quick break. I quickly took some medicine, gave some hugs, and kept trekking on.

I knew the only way out was through. I focused on one mile at a time, kept taking my gel, and tried to stay positive. Thankfully, that's the beautiful thing about a major marathon: There are reasons all around you to keep pushing. Everywhere I turned, I saw people of all ages, backgrounds, and abilities, with their own reasons for being out there. It was impossible not to feel inspired—or reminded that I was part of something so much bigger than my own race.It wasn’t until mile 22, shockingly on the Fifth Avenue climb to Central Park, where I had a breakthrough that felt supernatural—a combo of adrenaline and gratitude for what my body was doing. I was able to see my family one last time and then finish the race strong.

I was expecting to cross the finish line feeling so victorious, maybe even relieved that it was over. But I felt kind of disappointed. Not because of how I’d performed, but because this thing I’d spent months training for, thinking about, and looking forward to was suddenly over.

So let me be the first to tell you that what you get out of a marathon has very little to do with how fast you finish. And that's not to take anything away from people who work incredibly hard to get faster and faster in this sport. That's amazing! But the marathon is the great equalizer, and here's what I mean.

The marathon is hard for everyone. It doesn’t matter how fast you run it or whether it’s your first marathon or your 10th. There's a camaraderie that develops on the course because everyone is going through something so taxing together. You're all hurting, you're all pushing through, and your pace doesn't change that.

Plus, no two marathons are the same: different climates, crowds, elevations, and race-day challenges (like getting sick!). Your finish time cannot possibly capture everything you experienced out there. And it certainly doesn't always reflect the months of hard work you put into training or what you're actually capable of.

Then there's the sheer physical demand of the distance. A marathon requires so much time on your feet. You have to have a fueling strategy (salt…hydration…carbs…). No matter your pace, 26.2 miles is going to take it all out of you.

But maybe the most incredible part is that everyone out there is running for a different reason. You will see people out there of all ages, with limb differences, on wheels, with vision impairments, pregnant, pausing mid-race to breastfeed, after a weight-loss transformation. You name it. Everyone has a story, which makes the marathon one of the most inspiring environments you get to run in.

Bandit Running Women's Vanisher™ V-Neck Race Crop

My point with all of this: If you wait until you feel ready, you’ll be waiting forever.

If you want to get better at running, dabble in a 5K or 10K, or even a half-marathon. But if you want to learn who you really are deep within your core, sign up for the marathon. There's something about those 26.2 miles that shows you a side of yourself you might never have met otherwise. But you have to surrender to every mile and let the race have its way with you to truly transform.

And if you’re a busy mama like me—debating if you can, debating if it’s fair to the kids, debating if it’s the right time—there is no right time. There will always be a sick kid, a work deadline, a setback, a reason to wait. You aren’t promised to show up to the start line feeling your best.

Not because you have anything to prove. You get through bedtime every night without a crowd cheering you on or a medal waiting at the finish line. You're already doing hard things every single day. But sometimes you need to choose something hard just for you to remember what you're capable of.

And maybe the best part isn't even what you discover about yourself along the way. It's that your kids get to grow up watching their mom go after something she wants, even when the timing isn't perfect, even when it's hard, and even when it would be so much easier not to.

Lauren Kanski, CPT, is a member of the Women’s Health advisory board, a NASM-certified personal trainer, and a kettlebell and strength training specialist. She is the founding coach of the Ladder fitness app, where she leads Team Body and Bell, a strength training program focused on building muscle, power, and confidence. She lives in Colorado with her husband and two little ones.

To thwart reverse engineering by agents, dev proposes blinding them

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Now that LLMs can help analyze commercial software binaries and recreate their functionality, one software developer and student has created a project designed to make this process more difficult.

Timo Zhang's project is called Blind LLM Agents (BLA), and as its name suggests, it attempts to interfere with efforts by AI agents to scrutinize app code.

"Coding agents can reverse-engineer shipped packages, CLIs, and WASM in minutes," Zhang told The Register in an email. "Classic obfuscation was built for humans and static tools, not LLMs that reason about meaning. Blind LLM Agents is a protection layer for that threat model."

Such agents can also help developers build alternatives to existing commercial software.

Last month, Artcraft, a two-person development shop, released seven open-source apps that aim to be replacements for Adobe's commercial apps Photoshop, Illustrator, Premiere Pro, Lightroom, After Effects, InDesign, and Acrobat Pro. Artcraft claims these apps are "clean-room reimplementations" written from scratch in Rust using Anthropic Opus 5.5.

Setting aside the question of whether enlisting an AI model to reimplement an app really immunizes the creator from legal risk, those making apps face the possibility that someone will have an LLM probe published app binaries and reconstruct them through reverse engineering. There's in fact a framework for this, Reverse Engineer Anything (REA).

Zhang has faced some criticism on social media for creating a limited barrier to copying other people's work (which at this point might simply be the work of another LLM). But not everyone accepts that all publicly accessible binaries should be easily available for review and reuse. BLA cannot prevent committed reverse engineering efforts, but it may add a bit of friction to the process when LLMs are involved.

"It doesn't write app code — it hardens what you've already built," Zhang said. "A transform pipeline (plus MCP tools / an agent Skill) applies renaming, lane splitting, seed-derived string encryption, build polymorphism, WASM export renaming, decoys, self-seal integrity checks, and a coverage matrix that won't claim protection it can't prove. Behavior stays equivalent; recovery cost goes up."

Zhang said various evaluations like red teaming and CrackMe challenges to recover keys or flags from hardened artifacts show that BLA is effective.

"We also drive REA (a reverse-engineering agent) against those builds," he said. "Fail-to-break is the pass. CI tracks defense score, self-verifies integrity coverage, and checks functional equivalence. We raise the cost — we don't claim it is unbreakable."

The project website says as much, noting that it raises the effort for analysis and adds decoy code, but doesn't promise protection against traditional interactive reverse engineering tools like Ghidra or Frida.

Zhang said he's aware of other software projects that aim to interfere with AI, such as Poison Fountain for poisoning training data and Anubis for deterring bots and scraping. BLA, he said, is different. It's for hardening shipping code against reverse engineering and LLM-aided analysis. ®

A Skier Broke Both Legs in an Avalanche, Then Survived More Than 36 Hours in a Crevasse

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Members of France’s CRS Savoie mountain-rescue team search a snow-covered slope near Pointe Sommeiller in the French Alps after a skier was swept into a crevasse by an avalanche on November 8, 2026. (Photo: CRS Savoie)

A backcountry skier in France is lucky to be alive after an avalanche left him trapped in a crevasse for more than 36 hours with both legs broken.

The avalanche, which occurred in the French Alps, swept the skier into the crevasse on November 8, according to a Facebook statement from the French national police’s CRS Savoie mountain-rescue team. The skier crawled the length of a football field along a passage inside the crevasse, searching for a way out, before rescuers found him alive on November 9.

The 39-year-old, whose name has not been released, was on a solo backcountry ski trip near the French-Italian border. He was headed for the summit of Pointe Sommeiller, a 10,932-foot peak known for its steep, technical terrain, according to the French Alpine Club, when the avalanche hit.

The peak is located on the France-Italy border, 100 miles east of Grenoble.

Details of the rescue were shared in the official Facebook post, and also in a news story published by French newspaper Le Dauphiné Libére.

According to these reports, the skier left the Alpine village of Bramans on November 7 and spent the night at a backcountry mountain hut before setting out the following morning to climb Pointe Sommeiller.

At the base of a steep, snow-filled chute, he strapped his skis to his pack and began booting up the slope in crampons. His weight triggered a slab avalanche that swept him into a crevasse, fracturing his femur and breaking his other lower leg. Stranded without cell service, he ditched his gear and began crawling down a sloping passage inside the crevasse, hunting for an exit to the surface.

He eventually reached a chamber roughly 50 feet beneath the snow, but the slope leading out was too steep to climb with broken legs, Major Yoann Haberey of the CRS Alpes mountain-rescue unit told Le Dauphiné Libéré.

A concerned relative reported the skier missing late on November 8, prompting a search that continued until approximately 2 A.M., after the risk of another avalanche forced rescuers to suspend operations.

Crews returned the following morning, when a helicopter spotted signs of the avalanche. Search teams began probing and digging through the snow, gradually uncovering the man’s skis and backpack.

After more than five hours of excavation, rescuers finally heard his voice coming from roughly 50 feet beneath them.

The man was conscious but suffering from hypothermia when crews reached him. They extracted him using a stretcher and winch system, lowered him roughly 650 vertical feet down the mountain, and evacuated him by helicopter to a hospital in southeastern France.

The operation involved 22 officers from France’s CRS mountain-rescue unit, six military police rescuers, and three helicopters.

“He really is a miracle survivor,” Haberey told Le Dauphiné Libéré. “They were forecasting snowfall overnight, so after that, we weren’t going to go back.”

Following the rescue, the CRS Savoie mountain-rescue team urged people heading into the mountains to adjust their plans to current conditions, prepare their routes and equipment, and know when to turn back.

“The mountain will always be there tomorrow,” the rescuers wrote.

Where to catch Bloodgill in DREADMOOR

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

While attempting to fix all tuning forks in DREADMOOR, Malcolm will meet the enigmatic Father Matthias, who will task him with catching two Bloodgill fish if he wants to finish his quest.

The fish’s location, however, won’t be marked on your map, making it a matter of knowing where to search. To help you save both fuel and time, here’s where to catch Bloodgill in DREADMOOR, as well as what you must do after giving them to Father Matthias.

Contrary to what you might imagine, you won’t be able to find Bloodgill spawns close to Father Matthias’ island in DREADMOOR. Instead, you can catch the fish by heading to the spot marked below, located south of The Slough’s Trading Post. The fish will become way more common after you reach the Big Hollow, though, and will be easy to spot close to the post’s new location.

Like all other predatory catches, the Bloodgill can be angled with both the Rainbow Fin and Flaming Belly baits. You can check out the items needed to craft both below, as well as get the Flaming Belly recipe by purchasing it from Gill and Bozur for 80 Coins at the trading post. To see how to unlock all other baits, take a look at our How to get all bait recipes in DREADMOOR guide.

After giving the fish to Father Matthias, you can then progress through the quest by heading to the top of the island and placing both the tuning fork prongs and the other required items at its ritual site. Make sure to also sleep before heading back to the trading post to avoid the dangers of nighttime.

It’s A Relief To Go Back To The Simple Joy Of Ocarina Of Time After A Decade Of Being Overwhelmed By Breath Of The Wild

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the WildThe Legend of Zelda: Ocarina Of TimeThe Legend of Zelda: Tears of the Kingdom

My friends, put the tomatoes down. I havenât even gotten to speak yet. I, like most people who own a Nintendo Switch 2 and are killing time until Grand Theft Auto 6 launches next week, am playing The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time, and Iâm having a pretty good time going back to a game that was once an elementary school obsession for me, especially because itâs been a long time since I really felt a constant sense of progression in a Zelda game after a decade of Breath of the Wild and Tears of the Kingdom.

Yeah, the game is incredibly simple by modern standards, and Nintendo has done a lot to ensure that by making the game demonstrably easier in dozens of small ways that add up, but I think that relative simplicity is what draws me to it. Iâve been chipping away at the game in short sessions, usually finishing a temple or two before putting it down for the rest of the day, and the tangible feeling of momentum is what keeps me coming back to it just about every other evening at this point.

Over the years, especially with the rise of open-world games, I have identified that I am a goal-oriented person when it comes to how a video game can hold my attention. Thereâs just so much competition for whatever spare hours I have that if the bulk of my time playing a game is spent getting to the things I care about, I can lose interest fairly quickly. I want to see where the story goes, I want to reach the end of a quest line, and I loathe tedious open-world traversal more than most video game activities.Â

I canât entirely avoid open-world games in 2026, but if a game doesnât have a quick or engaging way to get around a giant map, it actively deters me from playing it. I often cite the Infamous series, with all its superpowered movement and interesting environmental interactions, as a great example of a game that makes its open-world map tolerable to me. A car or a horse is probably not going to cut it. There are exceptions from time to time, such as Cyberpunk 2077âs Night City entrancing me so much that Iâd gladly take in the view as I drove around on my motorcycle. But if I can help it, I want to spend my limited gaming time on things that arenât simply moving from one side of a giant space to another.

Breath of the Wild and its excellent sequel, Tears of the Kingdom do a lot to break up the tedium of simply moving through a space. The survival sim-esque friction and gizmo-building mechanics are brilliant evolutions of the original Zelda formula, and Nintendoâs ability to still draw water from the well of a decades-old franchise is commendable. I really enjoyed my time with both games, despite them grinding against a lot of my ingrained gaming sensibilities. The puzzles in the shrines, the constant feeling of discovery in its giant open world, and the colorful casts of weirdos and heroes you meet throughout the games maintain all that same Zelda spirit, but with a meaningful expansion of what a Zelda game looks like.Â

But man, is it a relief to go back to Ocarina of Time. I want puzzle-solving and discovery that I can get to quickly, that doesnât need me to meaningfully prep a survival bag so I can trek to the other side of the map while hoping my melee weapons can last me the whole trip, and what Ocarina is delivering feels downright breezy. Iâm sorry, Iâm sorry. I know itâs regressive of me to say Iâd like for the series to strip away all the cool things itâs done in the past nine years, but I havenât had a Zelda experience that feels like this in so long. The top-down 2D games are similar (at least those that came after the pretty open-ended and opaque original game), but they donât have the targeting and swordplay of the postâOcarina of Time era that rounds out the vision I have of what makes a 3D Zelda game.

I donât really hold any illusions that Zelda is going to go back to the structure of Ocarina of Time, but it is refreshing to be handed the tools I need to accomplish things without worrying that my weapon is going to break from battle damage or that Iâll run out of some vital consumable before I reach my goal. The Legend of Zelda is gonna be fine no matter where it goes moving forward, but for as dated and small in scope as Ocarina of Time might feel compared to Tears of the Kingdom, it sure does remind me that there was once a time when I didnât feel like I was fighting against the elements just to get to where I wanted to go.
